\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
.
To get
w
exactly, we construct
\mathbb{I}
so that
\val(\mathbb{I})
is
\val(\mathbb{I}^{\prime})
without its last letter. It is easy to see that this can be done in polynomial time (it follows, e.g. from
[
42
, Theorem 7.1]
).
∎

We are now ready to prove
\cref
thm:cfl-directed-hardness. Given a
grammar
G
and an SLP
\mathbb{B}
as in

\cref
lemma:membershipPSPACEhardness, we use
\cref
complement-ideal to
construct an SLP
\mathbb{I}
with

\mathtt{Idl}(\mathbb{I})\cap\Sigma^{|\val(\mathbb{I})|}=\Sigma^{|\val(\mathbb{I})|}\setminus\{\val(\mathbb{B})\}
.
Observe that now
\val(\mathbb{B})\notin L(G)
if and only if

L(G)\subseteq\mathtt{Idl}(\mathbb{I})
. Moreover, observe that
L(G)
is
finite and
\mathtt{Idl}(\mathbb{I})
is infinite. Therefore, the following
lemma implies that
\val(\mathbb{B})\notin L(G)
if and only if
the context-free language
L(G)\cup\mathtt{Idl}(\val(\mathbb{I}))
is directed, yielding

{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
-hardness.

Lemma 24
.

For finite
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
and an infinite ideal
I
, we have
L\subseteq I
iff
L\cup I
is directed.

Proof.

Clearly, if
L\subseteq I
, then
L\cup I=I
is an ideal and thus directed. Conversely, suppose
L\cup I
is directed. Consider an ideal decomposition
L\mathord{\downarrow}=I_{1}\cup\ldots\cup I_{n}
. Here, all
I_{i}
are finite since
L
is finite. Since
L\cup I
is directed, the downward closure
(L\cup I)\mathord{\downarrow}
must coincide with one of the ideals in the ideal decomposition
(L\cup I)\mathord{\downarrow}=I_{1}\cup\ldots\cup I_{n}\cup I
.
Since
I
is the only infinite ideal, this is only possible with
(L\cup I)\mathord{\downarrow}=I
. In particular,
L\subseteq I
.
∎

6
Downward closure comparison

Regular languages

We now show how to obtain

\cref
directed-nfa-dce as a byproduct of our results. For the upper bounds,
we use
\cref
nfa-ideal-candidate to compute, in
{\mathsf{AC}}^{1}
resp.
{\mathsf{NL}}
, a
candidate ideal
I_{i}
for each input language
L_{i}
. Since
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are
directed, we must have
L_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}=I_{i}
and we can decide in deterministic
logspace whether
I_{1}=I_{2}

[
49
]
. This yields an
{\mathsf{NL}}
upper
bound for fixed alphabets and an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{1}
upper bound for arbitrary alphabets.

For the
{\mathsf{NL}}
lower bound, we reduce from emptiness of NFAs: Given an NFA

\mathcal{A}
, we may assume that all transitions are labeled with the empty word

\varepsilon
. We take an NFA
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that just accepts
\{\varepsilon\}
. Then

L(\mathcal{A})\neq\emptyset
if and only if
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\mathord{\downarrow}=L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
, proving

{\mathsf{NL}}
-hardness.

Context-free languages

We now show
\cref
directed-cfl-dce. We first use
\cref
cfl-ideal-candidate to compute an SLP
\mathbb{A}_{i}
for
a candidate ideal for each
L_{i}
. By directedness,
L_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}=\mathtt{Idl}(\val(\mathbb{A}_{i}))
.
To decide
\mathtt{Idl}(\val(\mathbb{A}_{1}))=\mathtt{Idl}(\val(\mathbb{A}_{2}))
, we use the fact
that two reduced ideal representations yield the same ideal if and only if they
are syntactically identical
[
32
, Theorem 6.1.12]
. To check

\val(\mathbb{A}_{1})=\val(\mathbb{A}_{2})
we may apply the well-known result of
Plandowski
[
41
]
that equality of SLPs can be decided in
polynomial time (see also
[
39
]
).

For the
{\mathsf{P}}
lower bound, we reduce from emptiness of CFL: Given a
CFG
G
, we may assume that the only word
G
can produce
is the empty word (otherwise, just replace all occurrences of terminal letters
with the empty word). We also take a grammar
G^{\prime}
with

L(G^{\prime})=\{\varepsilon\}
. Then clearly,
L(G)\neq\emptyset
if and only if

L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}=L(G^{\prime})\mathord{\downarrow}
, yielding
{\mathsf{P}}
-hardness.

7
Conclusion

We have initiated the investigation of the directedness problem and
determined the exact complexity for context-free languages and for NFAs over fixed alphabets. Over variable alphabets, we show an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{1}

upper bound for NFAs. Despite serious efforts, we leave the
exact complexity open. Note that the complexity of directedness
is the same for DFAs and NFAs
\cref
sec:appendix-dfas. Also, the complexity of the maximum weight path
problem is not known
[
22
]
.

The developed techniques could be of independent interest.
The idea to analyze ideals by their weights might apply to other procedures for
reachability involving
ideals
[
25
,
26
,
16
,
18
,
17
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.
Furthermore, our
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
lower bound can be viewed as progress towards
resolving the complexity of the
compressed subword problem
: Our lower bound
applies in particular to deciding
L\subseteq I
for context-free
L
and a
compressed ideal
I
. Compressed subword, on the other hand, is equivalent to
deciding
I\subseteq J
for compressed ideals
I,J
. As mentioned before,
it is a long-standing open problem to close the gap between the
{\mathsf{PP}}
lower
bound and the
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
upper bound
[
38
]

(see
[
39
]
for a survey) for compressed subword.

The surprisingly low complexity of downward closure equivalence (DCE) for
directed CFL calls for an investigation of further applications of directed
CFL. As previously stated, safety properties of concurrent programs only depend on
the downward closure of the participating
threads
[
5
,
11
,
40
]
.
It is conceivable that deciding
safety
[
5
,
13
]
or
other notoriously difficult problems such as
refinement
[
10
]
are more tractable for directed
threads as well.

Appendix A
Proofs of Section
3

See
7

Proof.

If the representation
\and_{1}\cdots\and_{n}
is not in reduced form, there exists two consecutive atoms
\and_{i}
and
\and_{i+1}
that are absorptive. This implies we can merge
\and_{i}
and
\and_{i+1}
, by letting only the absorbing one to remain. It is easy to see that, this does not change the ideal that is represented while shortening the length of its representation. By induction on the length of the representation, it follows that every ideal has a reduced representation.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
4

See
10

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,\delta,q_{0},F)
.
By collapsing each strongly connected component of
\mathcal{A}
into one state, we obtain a partially ordered NFA
\mathcal{R}
that recognizes
L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
.

Formally, let
\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}\}
be the set of SCCs of
\mathcal{A}
, which can be computed in
{\mathsf{NL}}
. Then

\mathcal{R}=(\hat{Q}=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\},\Sigma,\hat{\delta},\hat{q_{0}},\hat{F})

where

•

\hat{\delta}(s_{i},a)=s_{j}
for all
q_{i}\in S_{i}
and
q_{j}\in S_{j}
with
\delta(q_{i},a)=q_{j}
,

•

\hat{q_{0}}=s_{i}
for the
i
for which
q_{0}\in S_{i}
,

•

s_{i}\in\hat{F}
for all
i
for which
F\cap S_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

Observe that
\mathcal{R}
is partially ordered with respect to the reachability order on
Q
.

Claim 25
.

L(\mathcal{R})=L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
.

{claimproof}

\mathbf{(L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}\subseteq L(\mathcal{R}))}
Let
q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}q_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}\dots q_{n-1}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}q_{n}
be a path of
\mathcal{A}
with
q_{n}\in F
and
w=a_{1}\ldots a_{n}
. All transitions remain in
\mathcal{R}
for some
s_{0}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}s_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}\ldots s_{n-1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}s_{n}^{\prime}
where
q_{i}\in S_{i}^{\prime}
and
s_{n}^{\prime}\in\hat{F}
. Thus,
w\in L(\mathcal{R})
.

\mathbf{(L(\mathcal{R})\subseteq L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow})}
. Let
s_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}s_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{2}}\dots s_{n-1}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}s_{n}
be a path in
\mathcal{R}
for some
s_{n}\in\hat{F}
and let
w=a_{1}\dots a_{n}
. The existence of the transition
s_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i+1}}s_{i+1}
in
\hat{\delta}
implies the existence of a transition
q^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i+1}}q^{\prime}_{i+1}
for some
q^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
and
q^{\prime}_{i+1}\in S_{i+1}
in
\delta
. Since
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
are SCCs, there exists a path
q^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{va_{i+1}v^{\prime}}q^{\prime}_{i+1}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}
. It follows that, there exists a path
q_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{1}a_{1}v_{2}\dots a_{n}v_{n+1}}q
for some
q\in F
. This gives
w\in L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
.

∎

See
11

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}=(\hat{Q},\Sigma,\hat{\delta},\hat{q_{0}},\hat{F})
. To construct

, we double the state set to

, let the initial state

and final states

. Let the incoming transitions of each node

be received by

and direct outgoing transitions of it to

. Turn all non self-loop transitions in
\mathcal{R}
to single atom transitions in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{idl}}
.

q
has a self-loop, then let
q
go to
q^{\mathtt{c}}
in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{idl}}

q
contains no self loops, let

.

(q,\varepsilon,q^{\mathtt{c}})\in\delta^{\mathtt{idl}}

4.

(q_{1},a,q_{2})\in\delta

q_{1}\neq q_{2}

Remark 26

(Complexity of the construction of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{L}}
–
\cref
subsec:reducingideals)
.

Recall that the size of
\Gamma
is polynomial due to
\cref
lemma:idealNFA. Then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{L}}
can be constructed in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
, since to add the outgoing edges for each
t
, we only need to go over each
\alpha\in\Gamma
and check if
t
contains
\alpha
, which takes at most
\log(|\Sigma|)
space.

Lemma 27
.

For a transducer
\mathcal{T}
, let
\mathcal{T}^{rev}
be the transducer obtained by reversing the edges and flipping the initial and final states of
\mathcal{T}
. For a word
w=a_{1}\ldots a_{m}
over the input alphabet of
\mathcal{T}
, let
w^{rev}
be the reversed word, i.e.
w^{rev}=a_{m}\ldots a_{1}
.
Then,
\{v\mid v^{rev}\in\mathcal{T}^{rev}(w^{rev})\}=\mathcal{T}(w)
.

Proof.

We will continue with the proof as if
\mathcal{T}^{rev}
has multiple initial states, which are the final states of
\mathcal{T}
. In fact we can easily set a unique initial state by adding a new state
q_{init}
to
\mathcal{T}^{rev}
and adding
\varepsilon-
transitions,
(q_{init},\varepsilon,\varepsilon,q^{\prime})
for each final state
q^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{T}
, to comply with our definition of a transducer.

(
\supseteq
).
For any word
v\in\mathcal{T}(w)
, where
v=\out(r)
and
w=\inp(r)
for a run

r=(q_{1},a_{1},b_{1},q_{2})(q_{2},a_{2},b_{2},q_{3})\cdots(q_{m},a_{m},b_{m},q_{m+1})
in
\mathcal{T}
, where
q_{1}
is the initial state.
Then
r^{rev}=(q_{m+1},a_{m},b_{m},q_{m})\cdots(q_{3},a_{2},b_{2},q_{2})(q_{2},a_{1},b_{1},q_{1})
is a run of
\mathcal{T}^{rev}
where
\inp(r^{rev})=w^{rev}
and
\out(r^{rev})=v^{rev}
. That is,
v^{rev}\in\mathcal{T}^{rev}(w^{rev})
, which shows
\{v\mid v^{rev}\in\mathcal{T}^{rev}(w^{rev})\}\supseteq\mathcal{T}(w)
.

(
\subseteq
).
This side follows from the observation that
\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{T}^{rev})^{rev}
.
∎

It follows from
\cref
lem:reversedtransducer that, for a given ideal representation
\and_{1}\cdots\and_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}(\and_{1}\cdots\and_{n})
is right-reduced and from
\cref
lem:right-normality that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}
preserves left-reducedness.
It immediately follows that the composition transducer
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}
, takes an ideal representation and prints out an equivalent reduced representation.

Lemma 28

(
[
43
]
)
.

Given a transducer
\mathcal{T}
and an NFA
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}(L(\mathcal{A}))
is a regular language. Furthermore, we can calculate an NFA that accepts the language in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
.

Corollary 29
.

Due to
\cref
remark:complexityoftrl,
\cref
lem:reversedtransducer and
\cref
lem:shallitregular, an NFA that accepts
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{idl}})
can be constructed in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
.

See
13

is in the language of. Then,

Due to the length of
{\mathfrak{w}_{\Delta}}^{m+1}\geq|\Delta|\cdot(m+1)
, there exists some (smallest)

and an infix

of
{\mathfrak{w}_{\Delta}}^{m+1}
of length

such that
w^{\prime}\in L(\beta_{k})
. The fact that
|w^{\prime}|\geq 2
implies that
\beta_{k}
is an alphabet atom and the fact that each infix of
{\mathfrak{w}_{\Delta}}^{t}
of length
|\Delta|
contains each letter in
\Delta
further implies that the alphabet of
\beta_{k}
contains
\Delta
, and therefore that
\beta_{k}
contains
\and_{i}
.
This implies that
{\mathfrak{w}_{\Delta}}^{m+1}\in\mathtt{Idl}(\beta_{k})
and thus we have
w_{\and_{1}}\ldots w_{\and_{i}}\in\mathtt{Idl}(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k})
, and since
k
is the smallest number that satisfies this condition,
j=k
. Therefore in both cases, the premise of
\cref
line:two holds.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to8.24pt{\vbox to8.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.12187pt\lower-1.69133pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.43 3.36 C 5.43 6.36 3 8.79 0 8.79 C -3 8.79 -5.43 6.36 -5.43 3.36 C -5.43 0.37 -3 -2.06 0 -2.06 C 3 -2.06 5.43 0.37 5.43 3.36 Z M 0 3.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
with
f(i)=f(i+1)=j
for some
a\in\Sigma
.
But if
\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{j}
is the shortest prefix for which
w_{\and_{1}}\ldots w_{\and_{i-1}}a\in\mathtt{Idl}(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{j})
, then clearly
w_{\and_{1}}\ldots w_{\and_{i-1}}aa\not\in\mathtt{Idl}(\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{j})
since
\mathtt{Idl}(\beta_{j})=\{a,\varepsilon\}
, which shows that
\cref
line:three holds.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Section
5

See
18

Proof.

For
L\subseteq{\mathsf{L}}(G)
, we denote by
\texttt{alph}(L)
the set of terminals occuring in
L
.
Since we are interested in calculating a representation of
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}
at the end, we can assume WLOG that the language generated by each nonterminal
A\in N
is downward closed.
Upon this assumption, we recite some observations by Courcelle.

We observe that any nonterminal
A
that produces itself twice, i.e.
A\to^{*}wAw^{\prime}Aw^{\prime\prime}
for some
w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{*}
, exactly produces
\texttt{alph}(A)
.
It is clear that
L(A)\subseteq(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
. To observe the other side it is sufficient to observe that for any
x,y\in(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
,
xy
and
yx
are both in
L(A)
.
If a nonterminal
A
produces itself twice, we write
A\Rightarrow^{2}A
.

If a nonterminal
A
produces a nonterminal
B
, i.e.
A\to^{*}wBw^{\prime}
for some
w,w^{\prime}\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{*}
, we write
A\Rightarrow^{1}B
. Clearly,
A\Rightarrow^{2}A
implies
A\Rightarrow^{1}A
.
In the following, assume no nonterminal produces itself twice. Take
A\Rightarrow^{1}B\Rightarrow^{1}A
. Clearly
B\Rightarrow^{1}B
. For all such
A,B
we write
A\equiv B
and create equivalence classes

A_{\equiv}=\{B\mid B\equiv A\}
.

Then we know from
[
23
]
that each equivalence class generates the same language and this language is defined as follows.

L(A_{\equiv})=(\cdot A)^{*}~\dot{A}\mathord{\downarrow}~(A\cdot)^{*}\quad\text{where}

\cdot A:=\bigcup_{X\in A_{\equiv}}\texttt{alph}(\texttt{l}(X)),\quad\quad A\cdot:=\bigcup_{X\in A_{\equiv}}\texttt{alph}(\texttt{r}(X))

We set
\texttt{l}(X),\texttt{r}(X)
and
\dot{A}
as follows,

•

Let
X\in A_{\equiv}
and
X\to w
be a production where
w\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{*}
contains no nonterminals that produce
X
. Then
L(w)\in\dot{A}
.

•

Let
X,Y\in A_{\equiv}
and
X\to wYw^{\prime}
be a production with
w,w^{\prime}\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{*}
. Clearly,
w,w^{\prime}
do not contain any nonterminals that produce
X
, since we assumed
X
does not produce itself twice.
Then
w\in\texttt{l}(X)
and
w^{\prime}\in\texttt{r}(X)
.

Lastly, for nonterminals with
A\not\Rightarrow^{1}A
, we have the trivial rule
L(A)=\dot{A}\mathord{\downarrow}
.

Remember that the ideals of
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}
are defined as a finite sequence of alphabet and single atoms.
Next we construct a CFG
G^{\mathtt{idl}}
that produces the language of the ideal representations of
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}
.

Let
N^{\prime}\subseteq N
denote the set of nonterminals in
G
that produces themselves once, and not twice. We fix a representative nonterminal from each equivalence class
A_{\equiv}
in
N^{\prime}
.
For each
A\in N
, if
A\in N^{\prime}
, we set
\bar{A}
to the representative of its equivalence class. Otherwise, we set
\bar{A}
to
A
itself.

Let
G=\langle N,\Sigma,P,S\rangle
. We follow the below steps to compute
G^{\mathtt{idl}}=\langle N^{\mathtt{idl}},\Gamma,P^{\mathtt{idl}},S^{\mathtt{idl}}\rangle
:

1.

For each
A\in N
, add
\bar{A}
, and for each
A\in N^{\prime}
additionally add a new nonterminal
\bar{\bar{A}}
in
N^{\mathtt{idl}}
.

2.

For each
A\in N
with
A\Rightarrow^{2}A
, add
\bar{A}\to(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
, and
(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
in
\Gamma
.

3.

For each
A\in N^{\prime}
, add
\bar{A}\to(\cdot A)^{*}\bar{\bar{A}}(A\cdot)^{*}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
and add
(\cdot A)^{*}
and
(A\cdot)^{*}
in
\Gamma
.

4.

For each
A\to a
in
P
for some
a\in\Sigma
, if
A\in N^{\prime}
add
\bar{\bar{A}}\to\{a,\varepsilon\}
, otherwise add
\bar{A}\to\{a,\varepsilon\}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
and add
\{a,\varepsilon\}
in
\Gamma
.

5.

For each
A\to BC
in
P
where neither
B
nor
C
produces
A
,
if
A\in N^{\prime}
, add
\bar{\bar{A}}\to\bar{B}\bar{C}
, otherwise add
\bar{A}\to\bar{B}\bar{C}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
.

6.

Add
\{\varepsilon\}
in
\Gamma
and for each
Y\in N^{\mathtt{idl}}
, add
Y\to\{\varepsilon\}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
.

7.

Set
S^{\mathtt{idl}}
to
\bar{S}
.

Complexity of the Construction.

We split
N
into subsets of nonterminals that
(i) produce themselves twice,
(ii) produce themselves and each other once, and
(iii) don’t produce themselves.
This computation takes only linear time, e.g. using depth-first search.
Computing
\texttt{alph}(w)
for a
w\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{*}
similarly takes only linear time.
In order to compute
\cdot A
and
A\cdot
for each equivalence class
A_{\equiv}
, we need to iterate through the productions and compute
\texttt{alph}(w)
and
\texttt{alph}(w^{\prime})
wherever applicable, which can again be accomplished in linear time.
Clearly, the remaining steps do not take more time either, since we only go through each production once and do a linear work.

In addition, note that the size of
\Gamma
is linear in
\max\{|\Sigma|,|P|\}
and the size of
N^{\mathtt{idl}}
is at most
2\cdot|N|
.

Correctness of the Construction.
We claim that
L(G^{\mathtt{idl}})
is an ideal decomposition of
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}
, i.e. the words accepted by the ideals in
\bigcup L(G^{\mathtt{idl}})
are equal to
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}
.
For this, we only need to show that our contruction mimics Courcelle’s. For nonterminals that produces themselves twice, the language they generate is
(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
by Courcelle’s construction.
Clearly, those words are exactly the ones contained in the ideal
(\texttt{alph}(A))^{*}
, which is the language the nonterminal generates, due to Step 2.
For nonterminals that do not produce themselves, Step 4 and 5 make sure that the production with
\bar{A}
in
P^{\mathtt{idl}}
generates ideals that capture exactly
L(A)
.
For terminals that produce themselves only once, we need to show that the production in Step 3 gives
(\cdot A)^{*}\dot{A}\mathord{\downarrow}(A\cdot)^{*}
. Since the atoms
(\cdot A)^{*}
and
(A\cdot)^{*}
clearly mimic the rule, we only need to show that
L(\bar{\bar{A}})=\dot{A}\mathord{\downarrow}
. But this is clear since
\bar{\bar{A}}
has the productions that exactly capture the productions of
A\to w
in
G
that do not produce
A
. Thus,
L(w)
is in
L(\bar{\bar{A}})
for all such
w
.
Since these are the only productions
\bar{\bar{A}}
mimics, and
L(\bar{\bar{A}})
is downward closed,
L(\bar{\bar{A}})=L(\dot{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
.

Ideal representations of
L(G^{\mathtt{idl}})
.

Observe that
G^{\mathtt{idl}}
is
acyclic
. Therefore, the language it generates is finite and the length of the computation tree that yields each ideal representation is at most
|N^{\mathtt{idl}}|
. Consequently, the length of each ideal representation is at most
3\cdot 2^{2\cdot|N|}
(due to the production in Step 3 branching 3 times, and in the other branching at most twice).
∎

Lemma 30

(
[
43
]
Theorem 4.1.5)
.

Given a transducer
\mathcal{T}
and a context free grammar
G
,
\mathcal{T}(L(G))
is a context free language. Furthermore, we can calculate a CFG that accepts the language in polynomial time.

The time complexity of
\cref
lem:shallitCFL follows from the proof of the theorem in the citation, due to morphism, inverse morphism and intersection with a regular language all being polynomial time decidable for context-free grammars.

Appendix D
Example for
\cref
subsec:PSPACEub

Example 31
.

Consider the grammar that produces ideal representations over the nonterminal alphabet
\{S,A,B,C\}
, terminal alphabet
\{\{c,\varepsilon\},\{d,\varepsilon\},\{a,b\}^{*},\{a,c\}^{*}\}
and the productions

\displaystyle S\to AA\mid BB

\displaystyle A\to BB\mid CB\mid BC

\displaystyle B\to\{a,b\}^{*}\mid\{d,\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle C\to\{a,c\}^{*}\mid\{c,\varepsilon\}

Assume the ideal representation being guessed is
\{c,\varepsilon\}\{d,\varepsilon\}\{a,b\}^{*}\{a,c\}^{*}
.
The leftmost derivation of this ideal representation and the memory content at each step of the computation are given below side-by-side. The lefthand side depicts the rules that are taken, and the righthand side depicts the memory content during that step.

\displaystyle S\to\mathbf{A}A\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\mathbf{A}A

\displaystyle S\to\mathbf{C}BA\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\mathbf{C}BA

\displaystyle S\to\{c,\varepsilon\}\mathbf{B}A\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\{c,\varepsilon\}\mathbf{B}A

\displaystyle S\to\{c,\varepsilon\}\{d,\varepsilon\}\mathbf{A}\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\{d,\varepsilon\}\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle S\to\{c,\varepsilon\}\{d,\varepsilon\}\{a,b\}^{*}\mathbf{C}\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\{a,b\}^{*}\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle S\to\{c,\varepsilon\}\{d,\varepsilon\}\{a,b\}^{*}\{a,c\}^{*}\quad\quad

\displaystyle S\to\{a,c\}^{*}

As illustrated, once we generate an atom, we store it only for one step and then drop it. This allows us to generate an exponentially sized ideal representation using polynomial space. Another way to view the derivation tree and the memory content is given in
\cref
fig:ideal-derivation-tree.
Here, the path that is kept in memory while guessing the atom
\{d,\varepsilon\}
is shown in red color.
That is, while guessing each atom, we keep the path from the root that leads to the atom, as well as the unexplored righthand side nonterminals of each rule that was taken on the path.

Since
G^{\mathtt{red}}
is acyclic and thus the length of the derivation tree is polynomial, the derivation procedure explained in
\cref
ex:ideal-derivation can be completed in polynomial space.
Thus, we can guess any ideal representation generated by
G^{\mathtt{red}}
, atom by atom, in polynomial space.

S
A
C
\{c,\varepsilon\}
B
\mathbf{\{d,\varepsilon\}}
A
B
\{a,b\}^{*}
C
\{a,c\}^{*}

Figure 2
:
Derivation tree of the ideal in
\cref
ex:ideal-derivation. The path that is kept in the memory while guessing the atom
\{d,\varepsilon\}
is given in red.

While we are guessing an ideal representation generated by
G^{\mathtt{red}}
as explained, we simultaneously keep and update a (binary encoded) pointer inside
\val(\mathbb{I})
. Assume the guessed ideal representation is
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{m}
where each
\alpha_{j}
is an atom.
While
\alpha_{j}
is guessed, the pointer keeps the length of the shortest prefix of
\val(\mathbb{I})
,
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{j-1}
embeds in.
According to
\alpha_{j}
, we update the pointer value.

The explicit computation is as follows: let
i
be the length of the shortest prefix of
\val(\mathbb{I})
that embeds
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{j-1}
. If
j=1
,
i
is taken to be
0
. Then we set

i^{\prime}=\begin{cases}i+1&\text{if $i=0$ or $\val(\mathbb{I})[i]$ is a single atom, }\\
i&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then we check whether
\val(\mathbb{I})[i^{\prime}]
contains
\alpha_{j}
. If it does, we keep the pointer value at
i^{\prime}
and move on to guessing the next atom. If it does not, we increase
i^{\prime}
by one and repeat the process. If
i^{\prime}=|\val(\mathbb{I})|+1
, we declare the word rejected by
\val(\mathbb{I})
. If the production of the guessed ideal has ended, we declare the word accepted by
\val(\mathbb{I})
.

\cref
lemma:Lohrey allows us to compute
\val(\mathbb{I})[i]
in polynomial time at each call.

Lemma 32

(
[
39
]
)
.

Given an SLP and an index
i
of the unique word
w
it generates, one can calculate the
i^{th}
letter of
w
in polynomial time.

Therefore, the inclusion
L(G)\mathord{\downarrow}\subseteq I
can be checked in
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
.

Appendix E
Counting ideals

See
6

Proof.

By #NFA, we denote the problem where we are given an NFA
\mathcal{A}
and a
number
n
(in unary) and are asked how many words of length
n
are
contained in
L(\mathcal{A})
. This problem is well-known to be

{\mathsf{\#P}}
-complete (more precisely,

{\mathsf{spanL}}
-complete)
[
4
]
. We show that
counting ideals is
{\mathsf{\#P}}
-hard by reducing #NFA to it. Given an NFA

\mathcal{A}
over an alphabet
\Sigma
, we construct in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
an NFA

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=L(\mathcal{A})\cap\Sigma^{n}
. Now clearly, the
decomposition into maximal ideals of
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\mathord{\downarrow}
is

I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{m}
, where each

I_{i}=\mathtt{Idl}({a_{1}}^{\mathchoice{\hbox to8.24pt{\vbox to8.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.12187pt\lower-1.69133pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.43 3.36 C 5.43 6.36 3 8.79 0 8.79 C -3 8.79 -5.43 6.36 -5.43 3.36 C -5.43 0.37 -3 -2.06 0 -2.06 C 3 -2.06 5.43 0.37 5.43 3.36 Z M 0 3.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to5.01pt{\vbox to5.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-1.28868pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.19 1.68 C 3.19 3.44 1.76 4.87 0 4.87 C -1.76 4.87 -3.19 3.44 -3.19 1.68 C -3.19 -0.08 -1.76 -1.51 0 -1.51 C 1.76 -1.51 3.19 -0.08 3.19 1.68 Z M 0 1.68}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to8.24pt{\vbox to8.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.12187pt\lower-1.69133pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.43 3.36 C 5.43 6.36 3 8.79 0 8.79 C -3 8.79 -5.43 6.36 -5.43 3.36 C -5.43 0.37 -3 -2.06 0 -2.06 C 3 -2.06 5.43 0.37 5.43 3.36 Z M 0 3.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to6.18pt{\vbox to6.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.09135pt\lower-1.38997pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4 2.35 C 4 4.56 2.21 6.35 0 6.35 C -2.21 6.35 -4 4.56 -4 2.35 C -4 0.14 -2.21 -1.65 0 -1.65 C 2.21 -1.65 4 0.14 4 2.35 Z M 0 2.35}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.15694pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to5.01pt{\vbox to5.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-1.28868pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.19 1.68 C 3.19 3.44 1.76 4.87 0 4.87 C -1.76 4.87 -3.19 3.44 -3.19 1.68 C -3.19 -0.08 -1.76 -1.51 0 -1.51 C 1.76 -1.51 3.19 -0.08 3.19 1.68 Z M 0 1.68}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.82639pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})
for some word
a_{1}\cdots a_{n}

of length
n
in
L(\mathcal{A})
. In particular, the number of words of
length
n
in
L(\mathcal{A})
is exactly the number of ideals in the ideal
decomposition of
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. This proves
{\mathsf{\#P}}
-hardness.

Let us now show that one can count the number of ideals in the ideal
decomposition of
L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
in
{\mathsf{\#P}}
. Suppose
\Sigma
is the
alphabet of
\mathcal{A}
. We first apply ideal
\cref
lemma:reducedidealNFA
to compute an NFA
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{red}}
over some
\Gamma\subseteq\mathtt{atoms(}\Sigma\mathtt{)}
.
Now our task is to compute the number of paths in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{red}}
that read

maximal
ideals, i.e. ones that are not strictly included in any
ideal. Our non-deterministic polynomial-time Turing machine
guesses a word
w
accepted by
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{red}}
, and thus an ideal

I=\mathtt{Idl}(w)\subseteq L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
. We want to count this ideal (meaning:
accept in this branch of the TM) if
I
is maximal, i.e. not strictly
included in any other ideal of
\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{red}}
. Thus, it remains to show that
we can decide in polynomial time whether
I
is maximal.

We exploit the fact that all paths contain reduced ideals. An
inspection of
\cref
lemma:embedding and the proof of

\cref
prop:weightfunc shows that it is easy to construct, in
polynomial time, a finite-state transducer
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
with input
alphabet
\Gamma
such that the pair
(u,v)\in\Gamma^{*}\times\Gamma^{*}

is accepted by
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
if and only if (i)
u
and
v
are reduced
ideal representations and (ii)
\mathtt{Idl}(v)\subsetneq\mathtt{Idl}(u)
. Therefore,

I
is maximal if and only if
w\notin\mathcal{T}(L(\mathcal{R}^{\mathtt{red}}))
. Since one can
construct (in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
) an NFA for
\mathcal{T}(L(\mathcal{A}))
, this can be checked
in polynomial time.
∎

Appendix F
Deterministic finite automata

For the directedness problem for NFAs, we have an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{1}
upper bound and an

{\mathsf{NL}}
lower bound. This raises the question of whether perhaps the exact
complexity can be determined more easily for deterministic finite automata
(DFAs). We sketch briefly that this is not the case: There is a simple
deterministic logspace reduction that takes an NFA
\mathcal{A}
and computes a DFA

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
L(\mathcal{A})
is directed if and only if
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
is directed.
Hence, the complexity does not depend on whether the input automaton is
deterministic.

Eliminating
\varepsilon
-transitions

The reduction proceeds in
two steps. In the first step, we reduce to an NFA without

\varepsilon
-transitions. This is easily done in an
{\mathsf{NL}}
reduction (even
while preserving the exact language, not just directedness), but we are
interested in a deterministic logspace reduction. Given the NFA
\mathcal{A}
over

\Sigma
, we pick a fresh letter
\#\notin\Sigma
and replace every

\varepsilon
-transition
p\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q
with

p\xrightarrow{\#}q
. Moreover, we add a self-loop
q\xrightarrow{\#}q
to
every state. For resulting NFA
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, observe that
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\mathord{\downarrow}
is the
set of all words in
(\Sigma\cup\{\#\})^{*}
such that erasing all
\#
yields a
word in
L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
. It is easy to see that
L(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\mathord{\downarrow}
is directed if
and only if
L(\mathcal{A})\mathord{\downarrow}
is directed. In particular, directedness of the
NFAs’ languages itself is not affected.

Achieving determinism

In our second step, we determinize. By the
first step, we assume that we are given an
\varepsilon
-free NFA
\mathcal{A}
.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}
has transitions
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
. Moreover, suppose transition

t_{i}
is labeled with
a_{i}\in\Sigma
. We obtain a new automaton
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
from

\mathcal{A}
by labeling each transition
t_{i}
with the regular language

\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}^{*}b_{i}a_{i}
, where
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\notin\Sigma
are
n

pairwise distinct fresh letters. The language accepted by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is defined in
the obvious way: For each transition
t
, it can read an arbitrary word from
the language that labels
t
. Let
K
be the language of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
defined in this way.

First, note that for
K
, one can easily construct a DFA in
{\mathsf{logspace}}
.
Essentially, the last letter in each block from
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}^{*}
tells it
which transition of
\mathcal{A}
to simulate when reading a letter from
\Sigma

afterwards.

Finally, it is easy to see that
K
is directed if and only if
L(\mathcal{A})
is
directed. We say that a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Sigma\cup\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\})^{*}
is a

padding
of
w\in\Sigma^{*}
if
w
is obtained from
w^{\prime}
by deleting all
occurrences of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
. Now if
L(\mathcal{A})
is not directed, then there
are two words
u,v\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u,v\in L(\mathcal{A})
and there is no
common superword in
L(\mathcal{A})
. But then
u,v
must have paddings
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
in
K

and clearly,
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
cannot have a common superword in
K
(otherwise, we
could delete all
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
from it to obtain a common superword for

u,v
).

Conversely, if
L(\mathcal{A})
is directed, we consider two words
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in K
. They
are paddings of words
u,v\in L(\mathcal{A})
and so there is a common superword

w\in\Sigma^{*}
, i.e.
u\preccurlyeq w
and
v\preccurlyeq w
. But
w
also has a padding
w^{\prime}
in

K
. Moreover, we can insert more occurrences of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
into
w^{\prime}
to
obtain a common superword
w^{\prime\prime}
for
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
. Thus,
K
is directed as
well.
